Time Series Forecasting: ARIMA and the Box-Jenkins Method JAH-PRED-000001 · 1

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Time series forecasting is the art of predicting the future from the past in order: given a sequence of measurements taken over time, find the pattern and extend it. The standard tool is the ARIMA model, published by George Box and Gwilym Jenkins in 1970, which combines autoregression, differencing, and moving averages into a single framework. It works because most time series carry their future in their past: today's value depends on yesterday's, the trend continues, and the seasonal pattern repeats. The method is still the baseline against which newer forecasting methods are judged, more than fifty years after it was published.

History

The Box-Jenkins method was published in 1970 in the book Time Series Analysis: Forecasting and Control, by George Box and Gwilym Jenkins. It gave forecasters a disciplined procedure: identify the model by looking at the autocorrelation and partial autocorrelation functions, estimate its parameters, check the residuals, and only then forecast. ARIMA stands for autoregressive integrated moving average, and its three numbers, p, d, and q, state how many past values, how many differences, and how many past errors the model uses. The method spread through economics, business, and engineering in the 1970s and 1980s, and it remains the standard classical approach, taught in every forecasting course and built into every statistical package.

Description

Time series forecasting is the art of predicting the future from the past in order: given a sequence of measurements taken over time, find the pattern and extend it. The standard tool is the ARIMA model, published by George Box and Gwilym Jenkins in 1970, which combines autoregression, differencing, and moving averages into a single framework. It works because most time series carry their future in their past: today's value depends on yesterday's, the trend continues, and the seasonal pattern repeats. The method is still the baseline against which newer forecasting methods are judged, more than fifty years after it was published.

The Problem

What is time series forecasting with ARIMA models and the Box-Jenkins method from 1970, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the series as a sequence of dated values. Step 2: difference the series until it is stationary, so its mean and variance no longer drift. Step 3: identify the orders p and q from the autocorrelation functions. Step 4: estimate the parameters, check that the residuals look like white noise, and forecast by extending the fitted model. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five quarterly values, 120, 135, 128, 150, and 142, against reference values of 122, 133, 130, 148, and 144, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical forecast. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"published": 1970, "mae_worked": 2.0, "rmse_worked": 2.0, "max_gap_worked": 2}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates and the design: Box and Jenkins published the method in 1970, and ARIMA's p, d, and q remain the standard notation. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps between the values and their references are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, giving a mean absolute error of 2.0 and a root mean square error of 2.0, with a largest gap of 2. The result that matters is the discipline: identify, estimate, check, and only then forecast, because a model whose residuals are not white noise is a model that has missed something. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.0, max_err: 2, n: 5, rmse: 2.0.

When It Applies

ARIMA applies wherever a series is stable, the horizon is short, and a transparent baseline is needed: sales, demand, prices, and any regular measurement with trend and seasonality. It applies as the first model to try and the standard to beat, and only where the residuals can be checked. It does not apply where the structure breaks, where the horizon is long, or where the forecast must explain why the future will differ from the past.

Limits

The limits are the limits of extrapolating the past. ARIMA assumes the structure of the series stays the same, so a break, a policy change, or a crisis will defeat it. It handles trend and seasonality but not the reasons behind them, so it cannot foresee a change that has no precedent in the data. It also degrades quickly with horizon: a one-step forecast may be good while a twelve-step forecast is a guess. The honest account uses ARIMA as a baseline and a short-horizon tool, and reaches for other methods when the structure is unstable or the horizon is long.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, which sum to 10, so the mean absolute error is 10 divided by 5, which is 2.0. The squared gaps are 4, 4, 4, 4, and 4, which sum to 20, so the mean squared error is 4.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 4.0, which is 2.0. The largest single gap is 2. These are deliberately simple numbers, chosen so the arithmetic is transparent: a constant error of 2 gives a mean absolute error of 2 and a root mean square error of 2, which is the baseline case every forecaster should be able to recognize. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind ARIMA is the science of dependence over time: a value today depends on the values before it, and that dependence can be measured by autocorrelation. Differencing removes the trend so the dependence can be seen clearly, autoregression captures the dependence on past values, and the moving-average part captures the dependence on past shocks. The science says the method works when the dependence is stable, which is true of many economic and business series over short horizons, and fails when the structure changes, which is why the residuals must be checked. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(120, 122), (135, 133), (128, 130), (150, 148), (142, 144)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRED000001:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(120, 122), (135, 133), (128, 130), (150, 148), (142, 144)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRED000001().run())

Linear Regression: The Oldest Prediction Machine JAH-PRED-000002 · 2

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Linear regression is the oldest prediction machine in statistics: fit a straight line to the data by least squares, and use the line to predict new values. It was built from two ideas, least squares, published by Legendre in 1805 and Gauss in 1809, and regression to the mean, discovered by Francis Galton in 1886 when he found that tall parents have children who are tall but less tall than their parents. The method is still the first tool a forecaster reaches for, because it is transparent, fast, and honest about its own limits: the line shows the relationship, the residuals show what the line missed, and the fit can be checked at a glance.

History

The history runs from Legendre's 1805 publication of least squares and Gauss's 1809 treatment, through Galton's 1886 study of heredity, in which he coined the term regression, to the twentieth century, when regression became the workhorse of economics, medicine, and the social sciences. The method's power is its simplicity: one slope, one intercept, and a clear rule for fitting them. Its honesty is its residuals: the gaps between the line and the data show exactly where the linear assumption fails, and the pattern of the residuals tells the analyst what to do next. The method remains the baseline for prediction, the first model fitted and the standard every fancier model must beat.

Description

Linear regression is the oldest prediction machine in statistics: fit a straight line to the data by least squares, and use the line to predict new values. It was built from two ideas, least squares, published by Legendre in 1805 and Gauss in 1809, and regression to the mean, discovered by Francis Galton in 1886 when he found that tall parents have children who are tall but less tall than their parents. The method is still the first tool a forecaster reaches for, because it is transparent, fast, and honest about its own limits: the line shows the relationship, the residuals show what the line missed, and the fit can be checked at a glance.

The Problem

What is linear regression for prediction, from least squares to Galton's regression to the mean, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the data as pairs of an input and an outcome. Step 2: fit the line that minimizes the sum of the squared residuals, which is the least-squares line. Step 3: examine the residuals for patterns, because a pattern means the line has missed something. Step 4: predict new outcomes from the line, with the residual spread as the measure of uncertainty. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five predicted outcomes, 10, 15, 20, 25, and 30, against actual outcomes of 11, 14, 21, 24, and 31, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical regression. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"legendre_year": 1805, "galton_year": 1886, "mae_worked": 1.0, "rmse_worked": 1.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: least squares in 1805 and 1809, Galton's regression in 1886, and the method still standard today. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps between the predictions and the actuals are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, giving a mean absolute error of 1.0 and a root mean square error of 1.0, with a largest gap of 1. The result that matters is the transparency: a constant error of 1 is visible at once, and the residuals tell the analyst the line is consistently off by the same amount, which is a fact worth knowing. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 1.0, max_err: 1, n: 5, rmse: 1.0.

When It Applies

Linear regression applies wherever a relationship is approximately linear and must be explained: pricing, demand, risk, and any first model of a new problem. It applies as the baseline every other model must beat, and only where the residuals can be checked. It does not apply where the relationship is curved, where the errors are not independent, or where prediction is mistaken for causation.

Limits

The limits are the limits of a straight line. Regression assumes the relationship is linear, the errors are independent, and the future resembles the past, and each assumption can fail. It is sensitive to outliers, it cannot capture interactions or curves without help, and it cannot distinguish causation from correlation: a line that predicts well may still describe no causal mechanism at all. The honest account checks the residuals, reports the fit, and never claims the line causes what it predicts.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, which sum to 5, so the mean absolute error is 5 divided by 5, which is 1.0. The squared gaps are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, which sum to 5, so the mean squared error is 1.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 1.0, which is 1.0. The largest single gap is 1. These are deliberately simple numbers, chosen so the arithmetic is transparent and the point is clear: a constant error is a bias, and a bias can be corrected by shifting the line. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind regression is the science of linear association: when two quantities move together, a line can summarize how much one moves when the other moves by one unit, and that summary can predict. The science says the line is only as good as the association: a strong linear association gives a useful line, a curved association gives a misleading one, and the residuals are the test. The method works because so many real relationships are approximately linear over the range that matters, and it fails gracefully, because the residuals announce the failure. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The result that matters is the transparency: a constant error of 1 is visible at once, and the residuals tell the analyst the line is consistently off by the same amount, which is a fact worth knowing. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 1.0, max_err: 1, n: 5, rmse: 1.0.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of a straight line. Regression assumes the relationship is linear, the errors are independent, and the future resembles the past, and each assumption can fail. It is sensitive to outliers, it cannot capture interactions or curves without help, and it cannot distinguish causation from correlation: a line that predicts well may still describe no causal mechanism at all. The honest account checks the residuals, reports the fit, and never claims the line causes what it predicts.

Specification

  • dimensions: Scope: linear regression for prediction, from least squares to Galton's regression to the mean. Worked example figures: {"legendre_year": 1805, "galton_year": 1886, "mae_worked": 1.0, "rmse_worked": 1.0}.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(10, 11), (15, 14), (20, 21), (25, 24), (30, 31)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Classification for Prediction: Logistic Regression JAH-PRED-000003 · 3

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Classification is prediction with a yes-or-no answer: will the customer leave, will the loan default, will the patient be readmitted. Logistic regression is the standard tool for it, fitting a curve that turns a weighted sum of inputs into a probability between 0 and 1. The term logistic regression was introduced by Joseph Berkson in 1944, and the method became the workhorse of medical and social prediction through the twentieth century. It survives because it is interpretable: each input has a coefficient, each coefficient has an odds ratio, and the whole model can be read and argued with.

History

The history runs from Berkson's 1944 paper, which named the method and showed its use, through the post-war decades, when it became the standard for medical risk prediction, to the present, where it remains the baseline classifier in medicine, credit, and marketing. The logistic curve solves the problem that a straight line cannot: probabilities must stay between 0 and 1, and the curve bends to keep them there. The method's interpretability is its strength: a doctor can read the odds ratios, a lender can explain a decision, and a regulator can audit the model, which is why it is still used where a black-box classifier would not be accepted.

Description

Classification is prediction with a yes-or-no answer: will the customer leave, will the loan default, will the patient be readmitted. Logistic regression is the standard tool for it, fitting a curve that turns a weighted sum of inputs into a probability between 0 and 1. The term logistic regression was introduced by Joseph Berkson in 1944, and the method became the workhorse of medical and social prediction through the twentieth century. It survives because it is interpretable: each input has a coefficient, each coefficient has an odds ratio, and the whole model can be read and argued with.

The Problem

What is logistic regression and classification for prediction, from Berkson 1944 to modern use, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the data as inputs with a binary outcome, 1 for the event and 0 for its absence. Step 2: fit the logistic model, estimating the coefficients that best separate the two classes. Step 3: convert each new case's weighted sum into a probability through the logistic curve. Step 4: apply a threshold, usually 0.5, to turn the probability into a prediction, and report the calibration. The demo runs a small worked example of the calibration step: it takes five predicted probabilities, 0.8, 0.3, 0.9, 0.6, and 0.4, against outcomes of 1, 0, 1, 1, and 0, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the calibration arithmetic, not a record of a historical classification. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"berkson_year": 1944, "brier_worked": 0.092, "mean_p_worked": 0.6}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Berkson named logistic regression in 1944, and the method remains the baseline classifier today. The worked example shows the calibration arithmetic: the squared gaps are 0.04, 0.09, 0.01, 0.16, and 0.16, which sum to 0.46, so the Brier score is 0.092, and the mean predicted probability is 0.6, with 5 hits out of 5. The result that matters is the calibration: a classifier whose probabilities match the frequencies is useful even when it is wrong, because its probabilities mean something. The demo prints these checked figures: brier: 0.092, hits: 5, mean_p: 0.6, n: 5.

When It Applies

Logistic regression applies wherever a binary prediction must be interpretable: medical risk, credit decisions, churn, and any classification where the reasons matter. It applies as the baseline classifier and the auditable one, and only where the probabilities can be calibrated. It does not apply where the boundary is curved, where the classes are heavily imbalanced without correction, or where the decision must be made without a stated threshold.

Limits

The limits are the limits of a linear boundary. Logistic regression assumes the log-odds are linear in the inputs, so it cannot capture curved boundaries or interactions without extra terms. It is sensitive to imbalanced data, where the rare class is swamped by the common one, and it needs the threshold to be chosen, which is a decision about costs rather than a fact about the data. It also cannot say why a case is near the boundary, only that it is. The honest account reports the calibration, the threshold, and the costs of each kind of error.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the Brier score. The five predictions and outcomes give squared gaps of 0.04, 0.09, 0.01, 0.16, and 0.16, which sum to 0.46, and 0.46 divided by 5 is 0.092. The mean predicted probability is 3.0 divided by 5, which is 0.6, and 5 of the 5 predictions were hits. A Brier score near zero means the probabilities were well calibrated; the worked example's score of 0.092 is the honest middle, a model that is usually right and sometimes wrong. Formal statement: Brier = (1/n) * sum (p_i - o_i)^2, where p_i is the predicted probability and o_i is 1 when the event happened and 0 when it did not. The equation states the calibration check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted probabilities and outcomes, squares each gap, and averages them for the Brier score.

Science Proof

The science behind logistic regression is the science of modeling odds: the log-odds of an event are modeled as a linear function of the inputs, which keeps the probabilities bounded and makes the coefficients interpretable as changes in odds. The science says the method works when the log-odds are approximately linear in the inputs, which is true often enough to make it the default, and fails when the boundary between classes is curved or the classes overlap heavily. The calibration check is the test: a model whose predicted probabilities match the observed frequencies is honest, and one whose do not is not. Scientific relation: A predicted probability is calibrated when events it calls likely actually happen often; the Brier score measures that gap, with 0 for perfect prediction.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
probs = [(0.8, 1), (0.3, 0), (0.9, 1), (0.6, 1), (0.4, 0)]
def compute():
    brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
    n = len(probs)
    out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Predictive Maintenance: Listening to Machines JAH-PRED-000004 · 4

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Predictive maintenance is prediction applied to machines: instead of servicing equipment on a fixed schedule or waiting for it to break, watch its condition, vibration, temperature, sound, and predict the failure before it happens. The idea is old, the practice is modern: sensors are cheap, data is plentiful, and the cost of an unplanned stoppage is high enough to justify the monitoring. It is the industrial descendant of the expert-system fault diagnosis of the 1980s, with learned models added alongside the rules, and it is now standard practice in manufacturing, energy, and transport.

History

The practice runs from the condition monitoring of the mid-twentieth century, when vibration analysis first let engineers hear a bearing failing, through the expert-system fault diagnosis of the 1980s, to the sensor-rich predictive maintenance of the 2010s, when cheap sensors and cloud storage made continuous monitoring affordable. The economics drive it: an unplanned stoppage costs far more than a planned one, and a failure caught early is a repair rather than a replacement. The method is now standard in factories, power plants, and airlines, where the data is plentiful and the cost of failure is high, and it is spreading wherever equipment is expensive and downtime is costly.

Description

Predictive maintenance is prediction applied to machines: instead of servicing equipment on a fixed schedule or waiting for it to break, watch its condition, vibration, temperature, sound, and predict the failure before it happens. The idea is old, the practice is modern: sensors are cheap, data is plentiful, and the cost of an unplanned stoppage is high enough to justify the monitoring. It is the industrial descendant of the expert-system fault diagnosis of the 1980s, with learned models added alongside the rules, and it is now standard practice in manufacturing, energy, and transport.

The Problem

What is predictive maintenance, condition monitoring, and the prediction of machine failure, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the sensor readings as a time series, vibration, temperature, pressure, and sound. Step 2: establish the normal pattern for each reading, and watch for drift away from it. Step 3: predict the remaining useful life from the drift, using the history of similar machines. Step 4: schedule the maintenance before the predicted failure, and record the outcome. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five predicted remaining-life values, 120, 90, 150, 80, and 110 days, against actual values of 118, 92, 148, 85, and 108 days, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical maintenance program. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_worked": 2.6, "rmse_worked": 2.8636, "max_gap_worked": 5}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and the dates: vibration analysis from the mid-twentieth century, fault-diagnosis expert systems in the 1980s, and sensor-rich predictive maintenance in the 2010s. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 2, 2, 2, 5, and 2, giving a mean absolute error of 2.6 and a root mean square error of 2.8636, with a largest gap of 5. The result that matters is the economics: a prediction that is off by a few days is still useful, because the choice is between maintaining early and failing unexpectedly, and a few days of error is cheap compared with an unplanned stoppage. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.6, max_err: 5, n: 5, rmse: 2.8636.

When It Applies

Predictive maintenance applies wherever equipment is expensive, downtime is costly, and degradation is gradual: factories, power plants, airlines, and fleets. It applies where the sensor data is reliable, the history is rich, and the maintenance can be scheduled. It does not apply where failures are sudden and without warning, where the history is thin, or where the cost of monitoring exceeds the cost of the failures it would catch.

Limits

The limits are the limits of any prediction from history. A failure mode never seen before cannot be predicted, a sensor fault can mislead the model, and a model trained on one machine may not transfer to another. The prediction also needs a decision: maintaining too early wastes money, maintaining too late wastes more, and the threshold is a judgment about costs rather than a fact about the data. The honest account reports the failures caught, the failures missed, and the false alarms, because all three are the cost of the system.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 2, 2, 5, and 2, which sum to 13, so the mean absolute error is 13 divided by 5, which is 2.6. The squared gaps are 4, 4, 4, 25, and 4, which sum to 41, so the mean squared error is 8.2 and the root mean square error is the square root of 8.2, which is 2.8636. The largest single gap is 5. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the maintenance program; the program was proved, or not, by its record of failures caught and failures missed. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind predictive maintenance is the science of degradation: machines wear in patterns, and the pattern shows in the sensor data before the failure does. Vibration rises as a bearing wears, temperature rises as a seal fails, and the trend is the signal. The science says the prediction is only as good as the history: a model trained on one machine's failures may not transfer to another's, and a failure mode never seen before is invisible until it happens. The method works where the degradation is gradual and the history is rich, and it fails where the failure is sudden and without warning. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The method is now standard in factories, power plants, and airlines, where the data is plentiful and the cost of failure is high, and it is spreading wherever equipment is expensive and downtime is costly.

Problem

What is predictive maintenance, condition monitoring, and the prediction of machine failure, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: take the sensor readings as a time series, vibration, temperature, pressure, and sound. Step 2: establish the normal pattern for each reading, and watch for drift away from it. Step 3: predict the remaining useful life from the drift, using the history of similar machines. Step 4: schedule the maintenance before the predicted failure, and record the outcome. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five predicted remaining-life values, 120, 90, 150, 80, and 110 days, against actual values of 118, 92, 148, 85, and 108 days, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical maintenance program.

The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.6, max_err: 5, n: 5, rmse: 2.8636.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of any prediction from history. A failure mode never seen before cannot be predicted, a sensor fault can mislead the model, and a model trained on one machine may not transfer to another. The prediction also needs a decision: maintaining too early wastes money, maintaining too late wastes more, and the threshold is a judgment about costs rather than a fact about the data. The honest account reports the failures caught, the failures missed, and the false alarms, because all three are the cost of the system.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(120, 118), (90, 92), (150, 148), (80, 85), (110, 108)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Weather Forecasting: From Richardson to Numerical Prediction JAH-PRED-000005 · 5

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Weather forecasting is the oldest large-scale prediction problem, and the most successful: every day, numerical models solve the equations of the atmosphere on supercomputers and predict the weather days ahead with skill that would have seemed impossible a century ago. The idea was Lewis Fry Richardson's, published in 1922: the weather obeys the laws of physics, so if you know the present state of the atmosphere and solve the equations forward, you get the future. The first computer forecast came in 1950, on ENIAC, by Jule Charney and his colleagues, and the skill has improved steadily ever since, by about a day of lead time per decade.

History

The history runs from Richardson's 1922 book, Weather Prediction by Numerical Process, which showed the method but could not compute it by hand, through the first successful computer forecast in 1950, when Charney, Fjortoft, and von Neumann ran a simplified model on ENIAC, to the operational forecasting of the 1960s and 1970s, and the founding of the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts in 1975. The skill improved as the models, the observations, and the computers improved together: satellites from the 1960s supplied the data, faster computers solved finer grids, and better physics captured more of the atmosphere. Today's forecasts are accurate days ahead in a way Richardson could only imagine, and the limit is chaos: the atmosphere is sensitive to small differences, so the skill fades with lead time no matter how good the model is.

Description

Weather forecasting is the oldest large-scale prediction problem, and the most successful: every day, numerical models solve the equations of the atmosphere on supercomputers and predict the weather days ahead with skill that would have seemed impossible a century ago. The idea was Lewis Fry Richardson's, published in 1922: the weather obeys the laws of physics, so if you know the present state of the atmosphere and solve the equations forward, you get the future. The first computer forecast came in 1950, on ENIAC, by Jule Charney and his colleagues, and the skill has improved steadily ever since, by about a day of lead time per decade.

The Problem

What is numerical weather prediction, from Richardson 1922 to the first computer forecast in 1950, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the present state of the atmosphere from observations, satellites, balloons, and ground stations. Step 2: solve the equations of motion, thermodynamics, and moisture forward in time on a grid. Step 3: run the model many times with slightly different starting states, to make an ensemble that shows the range of possible futures. Step 4: report the forecast with its spread, so the user knows how confident to be. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five forecast temperatures, 18, 21, 15, 24, and 19 degrees, against observed temperatures of 17, 22, 16, 23, and 20 degrees, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical forecast. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"richardson_year": 1922, "eniac_year": 1950, "ecmwf_year": 1975, "mae_worked": 1.0, "rmse_worked": 1.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Richardson 1922, the first ENIAC forecast 1950, and the European Centre founded 1975. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, giving a mean absolute error of 1.0 and a root mean square error of 1.0, with a largest gap of 1. The result that matters is the improvement: forecast skill has gained about a day of lead time per decade, so a five-day forecast today is as good as a four-day forecast ten years ago, and the gain comes from better models, better data, and better computers together. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 1.0, max_err: 1, n: 5, rmse: 1.0.

When It Applies

Weather forecasting applies wherever a physical system can be modeled and observed: weather, climate, ocean, and any prediction where the equations are known. It applies where the observations are dense, the physics is understood, and the uncertainty is reported. It does not apply where the system is chaotic beyond the horizon, where the observations are thin, or where a single deterministic forecast is presented as if it were certain.

Limits

The limits are the limits of chaos and of observation. The atmosphere's sensitivity to small differences means no forecast can be accurate indefinitely, and the ensemble spread is the honest measure of that limit. The observations are never complete, especially over the oceans, so the starting state is always an estimate. And the physics is approximated: clouds, turbulence, and surface processes are modeled rather than resolved, and the approximations are the largest source of error. The honest account reports the forecast with its uncertainty, and never presents a single number as if it were certain.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, which sum to 5, so the mean absolute error is 5 divided by 5, which is 1.0. The squared gaps are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, which sum to 5, so the mean squared error is 1.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 1.0, which is 1.0. The largest single gap is 1. These are deliberately simple numbers, chosen so the arithmetic is transparent: a constant error of one degree is a bias, and a bias can be corrected. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind weather forecasting is the science of the atmosphere as a physical system: the air obeys the laws of motion and thermodynamics, so the future follows from the present by calculation. The science says the limit is chaos: small differences in the present state grow with time, so the forecast's skill fades with lead time, and the ensemble spread measures that fading. The method works because the physics is known and the observations are dense, and it fails where the physics is approximated, as in clouds and turbulence, and where the observations are thin. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(18, 17), (21, 22), (15, 16), (24, 23), (19, 20)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Financial Forecasting: Random Walks and Risk JAH-PRED-000006 · 6

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Financial forecasting is the hardest prediction problem in wide use, because the thing being predicted reacts to the prediction: if a forecast says a price will rise, traders buy, and the price rises now, which destroys the forecast. Louis Bachelier's 1900 thesis modeled prices as a random walk, and Eugene Fama's efficient market hypothesis, published in 1970, drew the conclusion: if prices already reflect all available information, then no forecast based on that information can beat the market. The hypothesis is not a counsel of despair but a standard: a forecasting method must prove it can beat the market after costs, and most cannot.

History

The history runs from Bachelier's 1900 thesis on speculation, which introduced the random walk to finance, through the post-war development of portfolio theory and the capital asset pricing model, to Fama's 1970 statement of the efficient market hypothesis. The 1980s and 1990s brought the tests: momentum, value, and other anomalies that seemed to beat the market, and the debate about whether they were real or artifacts. The 2008 financial crisis showed the other side: risk models that assumed normal times failed when the times were not normal, and the forecasts that mattered most were the ones about risk, not about price. The field's lesson is that financial forecasting is mostly risk forecasting, and risk is where the models earn their keep.

Description

Financial forecasting is the hardest prediction problem in wide use, because the thing being predicted reacts to the prediction: if a forecast says a price will rise, traders buy, and the price rises now, which destroys the forecast. Louis Bachelier's 1900 thesis modeled prices as a random walk, and Eugene Fama's efficient market hypothesis, published in 1970, drew the conclusion: if prices already reflect all available information, then no forecast based on that information can beat the market. The hypothesis is not a counsel of despair but a standard: a forecasting method must prove it can beat the market after costs, and most cannot.

The Problem

What is financial forecasting, from Bachelier 1900 to the efficient market hypothesis, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the price history as a time series of returns. Step 2: test whether the returns are predictable, by checking autocorrelation and the performance of candidate signals. Step 3: build a risk model, estimating the volatility and the correlation between assets. Step 4: report the forecast with its uncertainty, and test it out of sample, after costs. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five predicted daily returns, 0.5, -0.3, 0.8, 0.2, and -0.1 percent, against actual returns of 0.4, -0.5, 0.9, 0.1, and 0.0 percent, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical forecast. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"bachelier_year": 1900, "fama_year": 1970, "mae_worked": 0.12, "rmse_worked": 0.1265}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Bachelier 1900, Fama 1970, and the 2008 crisis as the test of risk models. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 0.1, 0.2, 0.1, 0.1, and 0.1, giving a mean absolute error of 0.12 and a root mean square error of 0.1265, with a largest gap of 0.2. The result that matters is the standard: a financial forecast must beat the market after costs, out of sample, and the worked example's small errors are the arithmetic, not the proof, because the proof is in the out-of-sample test. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.12, max_err: 0.2, n: 5, rmse: 0.1265.

When It Applies

Financial forecasting applies where the signal is real, the costs are counted, and the test is out of sample: risk modeling, portfolio construction, and any prediction whose value is measured after costs. It applies as risk forecasting more than price forecasting, and only where the uncertainty is reported. It does not apply where the signal is fitted to the past without an out-of-sample test, where the costs are ignored, or where a backtest is presented as a forecast.

Limits

The limits are the limits of predicting a system that reacts to prediction. A signal that works will be traded until it stops working, a model fitted to the past will fail when the regime changes, and a risk model that assumes normal times will fail in a crisis. The 2008 crisis is the standing example: the models were right about normal times and wrong about the times that mattered. The honest account tests out of sample, reports after costs, and never presents a backtest as a forecast.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.1, 0.2, 0.1, 0.1, and 0.1, which sum to 0.6, so the mean absolute error is 0.6 divided by 5, which is 0.12. The squared gaps are 0.01, 0.04, 0.01, 0.01, and 0.01, which sum to 0.08, so the mean squared error is 0.016 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.016, which is 0.1265. The largest single gap is 0.2. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the forecast; the forecast was proved, or not, by its out-of-sample performance after costs. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind financial forecasting is the science of markets as information processors: prices move when new information arrives, and the information that is already known is already in the price. The science says prediction is possible only where the information is not yet in the price, which is a narrow window, and that risk is more predictable than return, because volatility clusters and correlations persist. The method works where the signal is real and the costs are low, and it fails where the signal is noise fitted to the past, which is the commonest failure in finance. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

History

The history runs from Bachelier's 1900 thesis on speculation, which introduced the random walk to finance, through the post-war development of portfolio theory and the capital asset pricing model, to Fama's 1970 statement of the efficient market hypothesis. The 1980s and 1990s brought the tests: momentum, value, and other anomalies that seemed to beat the market, and the debate about whether they were real or artifacts. The 2008 financial crisis showed the other side: risk models that assumed normal times failed when the times were not normal, and the forecasts that mattered most were the ones about risk, not about price. The field's lesson is that financial forecasting is mostly risk forecasting, and risk is where the models earn their keep.

Problem

What is financial forecasting, from Bachelier 1900 to the efficient market hypothesis, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: take the price history as a time series of returns. Step 2: test whether the returns are predictable, by checking autocorrelation and the performance of candidate signals. Step 3: build a risk model, estimating the volatility and the correlation between assets. Step 4: report the forecast with its uncertainty, and test it out of sample, after costs. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five predicted daily returns, 0.5, -0.3, 0.8, 0.2, and -0.1 percent, against actual returns of 0.4, -0.5, 0.9, 0.1, and 0.0 percent, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical forecast.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.5, 0.4), (-0.3, -0.5), (0.8, 0.9), (0.2, 0.1), (-0.1, 0.0)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Epidemiology Forecasting: SIR Models and Outbreak Prediction JAH-PRED-000007 · 7

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Epidemic forecasting is prediction where the stakes are lives: given the first cases of an outbreak, predict how many will fall ill, when the peak will come, and what will slow it down. The standard tool is the SIR model, published by Kermack and McKendrick in 1927, which divides a population into the susceptible, the infectious, and the recovered, and tracks how people move between the groups. The model gave the field its central number, the basic reproduction number, and it was the workhorse of the COVID-19 forecasts of 2020, when governments needed to know what would happen next and what would change it.

History

The history runs from Kermack and McKendrick's 1927 paper, which founded the mathematical theory of epidemics, through the twentieth century, when the models guided vaccination and control, to 2020, when the COVID-19 pandemic made epidemic forecasting a matter of daily public attention. The 2020 forecasts varied widely, because the models depended on numbers that were not yet known, the reproduction number, the infection fatality rate, and the effect of interventions, and the variation was itself informative: it showed which numbers mattered and how much they mattered. The field learned to report the range, to update as data arrived, and to say plainly which assumptions drove the result.

Description

Epidemic forecasting is prediction where the stakes are lives: given the first cases of an outbreak, predict how many will fall ill, when the peak will come, and what will slow it down. The standard tool is the SIR model, published by Kermack and McKendrick in 1927, which divides a population into the susceptible, the infectious, and the recovered, and tracks how people move between the groups. The model gave the field its central number, the basic reproduction number, and it was the workhorse of the COVID-19 forecasts of 2020, when governments needed to know what would happen next and what would change it.

The Problem

What is epidemic forecasting with SIR compartmental models, from Kermack and McKendrick 1927 to COVID-19, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the early case counts and estimate the reproduction number, the average number of new infections each case causes. Step 2: fit the SIR model, with the population divided into susceptible, infectious, and recovered. Step 3: run the model forward under different assumptions about interventions, and report the range of outcomes. Step 4: update the forecast as new data arrives, and say which assumption drives the result. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five predicted weekly case counts, 1200, 1800, 2400, 2100, and 1600, against actual counts of 1150, 1850, 2350, 2150, and 1550, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical epidemic forecast. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"sir_year": 1927, "mae_worked": 50.0, "rmse_worked": 50.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Kermack and McKendrick 1927, and the COVID-19 forecasts of 2020. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 50, 50, 50, 50, and 50, giving a mean absolute error of 50.0 and a root mean square error of 50.0, with a largest gap of 50. The result that matters is the range: a forecast that reports a single number without its range is misleading, because the range is where the uncertainty lives, and the 2020 experience showed that the range was wide and that saying so was the honest thing to do. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 50.0, max_err: 50, n: 5, rmse: 50.0.

When It Applies

Epidemic forecasting applies wherever an outbreak must be anticipated and the assumptions can be stated: public health planning, hospital capacity, and intervention timing. It applies where the data is collected, the range is reported, and the forecast is updated as data arrives. It does not apply where a single number is presented as certain, where the assumptions are hidden, or where the forecast is used to justify a decision it cannot support.

Limits

The limits are the limits of predicting a system that includes human behaviour. People change their behaviour when they hear a forecast, which changes the forecast, and the data that feeds the model is always incomplete, because not every case is counted. The reproduction number is estimated, not measured, and a small error in it grows into a large error in the peak. The honest account reports the range, updates often, and says which assumption drives the result, because a forecast without its assumptions is a number without a meaning.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 50, 50, 50, 50, and 50, which sum to 250, so the mean absolute error is 250 divided by 5, which is 50.0. The squared gaps are 2500, 2500, 2500, 2500, and 2500, which sum to 12500, so the mean squared error is 2500.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 2500.0, which is 50.0. The largest single gap is 50. These are deliberately simple numbers, chosen so the arithmetic is transparent: a constant error of 50 cases is a bias, and a bias can be corrected by recalibrating the model. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind epidemic forecasting is the science of transmission: an outbreak grows when each case infects more than one other, and it shrinks when each case infects fewer than one, so the reproduction number is the whole story in a single number. The SIR model captures that story with three compartments and two rates, infection and recovery, and its predictions follow from those rates. The science says the model works when the rates are known and the population mixes evenly, and fails when the rates are unknown, when the mixing is uneven, or when behaviour changes in response to the forecast itself. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. import hashlib
pairs = [(1200, 1150), (1800, 1850), (2400, 2350), (2100, 2150), (1600, 1550)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Demand Forecasting: Retail, Supply Chains, and Holt-Winters JAH-PRED-000008 · 8

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Demand forecasting is prediction where the answer becomes a purchase order: given the history of what sold, predict what will sell, so the shelves are stocked and the warehouse is not. The standard classical tool is Holt-Winters exponential smoothing, published in 1960, which tracks the level, the trend, and the season of a series and extends them forward. It works because demand has a shape: a base level, a drift, and a seasonal pattern that repeats, and the method captures all three with a few numbers that can be updated as new sales arrive. Modern retail runs on its descendants, and the cost of a bad forecast is counted in stockouts and in warehouses full of unsold goods.

History

The history runs from the exponential smoothing of the 1950s, through Holt's 1957 work on trend and Winters' 1960 addition of seasonality, to the supply-chain forecasting of the 1970s and 1980s, when the methods became standard in retail and manufacturing. The 1990s and 2000s added scanner data, which gave forecasters daily sales by item and store, and the 2010s added machine learning, which found patterns the classical methods missed. The method's endurance is the evidence: Holt-Winters is still the baseline in forecasting competitions, because it is simple, fast, and hard to beat on series with stable seasonality.

Description

Demand forecasting is prediction where the answer becomes a purchase order: given the history of what sold, predict what will sell, so the shelves are stocked and the warehouse is not. The standard classical tool is Holt-Winters exponential smoothing, published in 1960, which tracks the level, the trend, and the season of a series and extends them forward. It works because demand has a shape: a base level, a drift, and a seasonal pattern that repeats, and the method captures all three with a few numbers that can be updated as new sales arrive. Modern retail runs on its descendants, and the cost of a bad forecast is counted in stockouts and in warehouses full of unsold goods.

The Problem

What is demand forecasting for retail and supply chains, from Holt-Winters 1960 to modern practice, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the sales history as a series by item and store. Step 2: estimate the level, the trend, and the seasonal factors from the history. Step 3: extend the three components forward to the horizon, and convert the forecast into orders. Step 4: measure the error when the actuals arrive, and update the components. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five predicted weekly sales, 420, 480, 390, 510, and 450 units, against actual sales of 415, 485, 395, 505, and 455 units, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical sales forecast. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"holt_year": 1957, "winters_year": 1960, "mae_worked": 5.0, "rmse_worked": 5.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Holt 1957, Winters 1960, and the methods still standard today. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 5, 5, 5, 5, and 5, giving a mean absolute error of 5.0 and a root mean square error of 5.0, with a largest gap of 5. The result that matters is the economics: a forecast that is off by five units on a base of four hundred is a good forecast, and the cost of the error is the cost of holding a little extra stock, which is small compared with the cost of a stockout. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 5.0, max_err: 5, n: 5, rmse: 5.0.

When It Applies

Demand forecasting applies wherever sales history is plentiful and the pattern is stable: retail, manufacturing, and supply chains. It applies as the baseline forecast and the standard to beat, and only where the error is measured and the breaks are flagged. It does not apply where the pattern breaks often, where the history is thin, or where a single point forecast is used without its error.

Limits

The limits are the limits of extrapolating a pattern. A promotion, a price change, a new competitor, or a crisis will break the pattern, and the method will keep forecasting the old pattern until the data forces it to adapt. The forecast also degrades with horizon, and it cannot foresee a demand that has no precedent in the history. The honest account pairs the statistical forecast with the forecaster's judgment about the breaks, and reports the error so the cost of the breaks can be counted.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 5, 5, 5, 5, and 5, which sum to 25, so the mean absolute error is 25 divided by 5, which is 5.0. The squared gaps are 25, 25, 25, 25, and 25, which sum to 125, so the mean squared error is 25.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 25.0, which is 5.0. The largest single gap is 5. These are deliberately simple numbers, chosen so the arithmetic is transparent: a constant error of five units is a bias, and a bias can be corrected by adjusting the level. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind demand forecasting is the science of stable patterns: demand has a level, a trend, and a season, and those three components change slowly enough to be estimated from history. Exponential smoothing works because it weights recent observations more than old ones, so it adapts as the pattern drifts, and it is honest because its errors are visible in the residuals. The science says the method works when the pattern is stable and fails when it breaks, as in a promotion, a price change, or a crisis, and the forecaster's job is to know which case applies. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(420, 415), (480, 485), (390, 395), (510, 505), (450, 455)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class ProgJAHPRED000008:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(420, 415), (480, 485), (390, 395), (510, 505), (450, 455)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}

Churn Prediction: Who Will Leave JAH-PRED-000009 · 9

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Churn prediction is forecasting with a business consequence: given a customer's history, predict whether they will leave, so the company can act before they do. It matters because keeping a customer is cheaper than finding a new one, and because the prediction is actionable: a customer flagged as likely to leave can be offered a reason to stay. The methods are classification and survival analysis, and the data is the customer's usage, payments, and contacts with support. The field grew up in telecom, where contracts and switching made churn visible, and it now runs in every subscription business.

History

The history runs from the telecom churn work of the 1990s, when carriers first modeled which customers would leave at contract end, through the 2000s, when the methods spread to banking, insurance, and retail, to the present, where subscription businesses run churn models continuously. The data is the customer's behaviour: falling usage, late payments, complaints, and contact with competitors are the signals, and the model turns them into a probability of leaving. The practice is now standard, and the honest account of it reports the costs: a false alarm wastes a retention offer, and a missed churner is a customer lost.

Description

Churn prediction is forecasting with a business consequence: given a customer's history, predict whether they will leave, so the company can act before they do. It matters because keeping a customer is cheaper than finding a new one, and because the prediction is actionable: a customer flagged as likely to leave can be offered a reason to stay. The methods are classification and survival analysis, and the data is the customer's usage, payments, and contacts with support. The field grew up in telecom, where contracts and switching made churn visible, and it now runs in every subscription business.

The Problem

What is customer churn prediction, from telecom retention to modern subscription analytics, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take each customer's history as features, usage, payments, tenure, and support contacts. Step 2: fit a classifier or survival model that turns the features into a probability of leaving. Step 3: rank the customers by probability, and flag the top of the list for retention action. Step 4: measure the outcome, and update the model as the customer base changes. The demo runs a small worked example of the calibration step: it takes five predicted churn probabilities, 0.7, 0.2, 0.85, 0.45, and 0.6, against outcomes of 1, 0, 1, 0, and 1, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the calibration arithmetic, not a record of a historical churn program. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"brier_worked": 0.103, "mean_p_worked": 0.56}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and the dates: telecom churn modeling from the 1990s, and the methods now standard in subscription businesses. The worked example shows the calibration arithmetic: the squared gaps are 0.09, 0.04, 0.0225, 0.2025, and 0.16, which sum to 0.515, so the Brier score is 0.103, and the mean predicted probability is 0.56, with 5 hits out of 5. The result that matters is the action: a ranked list is only useful if the top of the list is acted on, and the value of the model is measured in customers kept, not in accuracy alone. The demo prints these checked figures: brier: 0.103, hits: 5, mean_p: 0.56, n: 5.

When It Applies

Churn prediction applies wherever customers can leave and keeping them is cheaper than finding new ones: telecom, banking, insurance, and subscription businesses. It applies where the behavioural data is rich, the probabilities are calibrated, and the retention action is ready. It does not apply where the reasons for leaving are invisible in the data, where the model is not retrained as the base changes, or where the retention offer goes to customers who would have stayed.

Limits

The limits are the limits of predicting a human decision. Customers leave for reasons the data cannot see, a price rise, a move, a competitor's offer, and no model can foresee those. The model also decays as the customer base changes, so it must be retrained, and its predictions can become self-defeating if the retention offer goes to customers who would have stayed anyway. The honest account reports the customers kept, the offers wasted, and the churners missed, because all three are the cost of the system.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the Brier score. The five predictions and outcomes give squared gaps of 0.09, 0.04, 0.0225, 0.2025, and 0.16, which sum to 0.515, and 0.515 divided by 5 is 0.103. The mean predicted probability is 2.8 divided by 5, which is 0.56, and 5 of the 5 predictions were hits. A Brier score near zero means the probabilities were well calibrated; the worked example's score of 0.103 is the honest middle, a model that is usually right and sometimes wrong. Formal statement: Brier = (1/n) * sum (p_i - o_i)^2, where p_i is the predicted probability and o_i is 1 when the event happened and 0 when it did not. The equation states the calibration check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted probabilities and outcomes, squares each gap, and averages them for the Brier score.

Science Proof

The science behind churn prediction is the science of behaviour as a signal: falling usage, late payment, and complaints are not random, they are the visible part of a decision to leave, and the model reads them before the decision is final. The science says the signal is real but noisy, because customers leave for reasons the data cannot see, and the model must be judged by whether it finds the leavers early enough to act, not by whether it is right on every case. The calibration check is the test: probabilities that match the frequencies are useful, and ones that do not are not. Scientific relation: A predicted probability is calibrated when events it calls likely actually happen often; the Brier score measures that gap, with 0 for perfect prediction.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
probs = [(0.7, 1), (0.2, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.45, 0), (0.6, 1)]
def compute():
    brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
    n = len(probs)
    out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        import hashlib
        probs = [(0.7, 1), (0.2, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.45, 0), (0.6, 1)]
        def compute():
            brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
            mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
            hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
            n = len(probs)
            out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRED000009().run())
