Survival Analysis: Predicting Time Until an Event JAH-PRED-000019 · 19

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Survival analysis is prediction of when, not whether: given a patient, a customer, or a machine, predict how long until the event, the relapse, the churn, the failure. The standard tool is the Cox proportional hazards model, published by David Cox in 1972, which estimates how each factor multiplies the hazard, the instantaneous risk of the event. The Kaplan-Meier estimator, published in 1958, gives the survival curve without assuming a model at all. The field matters because so many decisions depend on timing: a treatment is judged by survival, a customer by tenure, and a machine by life.

History

The history runs from the Kaplan-Meier estimator of 1958, through Cox's 1972 model, which made survival analysis practical by separating the baseline hazard from the effects of the covariates, to the present, where the methods are standard in medicine, engineering, and business. The Cox model's power is its semi-parametric form: it estimates the effects without specifying the shape of the baseline hazard, which makes it robust. The methods spread wherever time-to-event matters, and they remain the standard, because they handle censoring, the fact that some subjects are still event-free when the study ends, which ordinary regression cannot.

Description

Survival analysis is prediction of when, not whether: given a patient, a customer, or a machine, predict how long until the event, the relapse, the churn, the failure. The standard tool is the Cox proportional hazards model, published by David Cox in 1972, which estimates how each factor multiplies the hazard, the instantaneous risk of the event. The Kaplan-Meier estimator, published in 1958, gives the survival curve without assuming a model at all. The field matters because so many decisions depend on timing: a treatment is judged by survival, a customer by tenure, and a machine by life.

The Problem

What is survival analysis, the Cox model of 1972, and prediction of time until an event, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take each subject's history as features and an observed time, with a flag for whether the event happened or the observation was censored. Step 2: estimate the survival curve with Kaplan-Meier, and fit the Cox model for the effects of the features. Step 3: predict each new subject's survival curve from the model. Step 4: measure the prediction's accuracy, and report the concordance, the share of pairs the model orders correctly. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five predicted survival times, 24, 36, 18, 48, and 30 months, against observed times of 22, 38, 20, 45, and 32 months, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical study. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"kaplan_meier_year": 1958, "cox_year": 1972, "mae_worked": 2.2, "rmse_worked": 2.2361}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Kaplan-Meier 1958, and the Cox model 1972. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 2, 2, 2, 3, and 2, giving a mean absolute error of 2.2 and a root mean square error of 2.2361, with a largest gap of 3. The result that matters is the interpretation: a mean error of about two months on survivals of eighteen to forty-eight months is a useful prediction, and the largest gap of three months is the one to investigate, because it may mark a case the model misunderstood. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.2, max_err: 3, n: 5, rmse: 2.2361.

When It Applies

Survival analysis applies wherever time until an event must be predicted: medicine, engineering, and customer tenure. It applies where the censoring is handled, the assumptions are checked, and the prediction is reported as a curve. It does not apply where the hazards are not proportional without correction, where the censoring is informative, or where a single predicted time is presented as if it were certain.

Limits

The limits are the limits of modeling time. The Cox model assumes the hazards are proportional, which may not hold, and it assumes the censoring is uninformative, which may not hold either. A survival prediction is a curve, not a number, and reducing it to a single time loses the uncertainty. The honest account reports the curve, checks the proportional-hazards assumption, and says how the censoring was handled, because a survival analysis that hides its censoring is not an analysis at all.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 2, 2, 3, and 2, which sum to 11, so the mean absolute error is 11 divided by 5, which is 2.2. The squared gaps are 4, 4, 4, 9, and 4, which sum to 25, so the mean squared error is 5.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 5.0, which is 2.2361. The largest single gap is 3. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the study; the study was proved, or not, by its concordance and its calibration. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind survival analysis is the science of time-to-event with censoring: some subjects are still event-free when the study ends, and their information is that they survived at least that long, which is partial information that must be used rather than discarded. The Cox model uses it by modeling the hazard, the instantaneous risk, and the Kaplan-Meier estimator uses it by updating the survival curve only at observed events. The science says the methods work when the censoring is unrelated to the risk, and fail when the subjects who leave the study are the ones at highest risk, which is the informative-censoring problem. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(24, 22), (36, 38), (18, 20), (48, 45), (30, 32)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Anomaly Detection: Predicting the Rare Event JAH-PRED-000020 · 20

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Anomaly detection is prediction of the rare and the wrong: given the normal pattern, flag the case that does not fit, because the misfit may be fraud, a fault, or an attack. It matters because the rare event is the expensive one, and because it cannot be learned from examples, since there are too few of them. The methods are statistical, distance-based, and learned, and they all share the same problem: the boundary between normal and anomalous must be drawn, and where it is drawn decides the trade between false alarms and missed events. The field is the prediction of the exception, and its honesty is measured in the false-alarm rate.

History

The history runs from the statistical quality control of the early twentieth century, through the fraud-detection systems of the 1980s and 1990s, to the present, where anomaly detection runs in payment networks, in factories, and in computer networks. The methods changed, from control charts to isolation forests to autoencoders, but the problem did not: normal is plentiful and anomalous is rare, so the model must learn normal and flag the rest. The practice is now standard, and the honest account of it reports the false alarms alongside the catches, because a system that cries wolf is soon ignored.

Description

Anomaly detection is prediction of the rare and the wrong: given the normal pattern, flag the case that does not fit, because the misfit may be fraud, a fault, or an attack. It matters because the rare event is the expensive one, and because it cannot be learned from examples, since there are too few of them. The methods are statistical, distance-based, and learned, and they all share the same problem: the boundary between normal and anomalous must be drawn, and where it is drawn decides the trade between false alarms and missed events. The field is the prediction of the exception, and its honesty is measured in the false-alarm rate.

The Problem

What is anomaly detection, the prediction of rare events from fraud to equipment failure, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the normal data and learn its pattern, the typical values and their variation. Step 2: score each new case by how far it lies from the normal pattern. Step 3: apply a threshold, and flag the cases above it as anomalies. Step 4: measure the outcome, the catches, the false alarms, and the misses, and adjust the threshold. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step: it takes five anomaly scores, 0.95, 0.4, 0.88, 0.3, and 0.72, against outcomes of 1, 0, 1, 0, and 1, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the calibration arithmetic, not a record of a historical detection system. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"brier_worked": 0.0691, "mean_p_worked": 0.65}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and the dates: statistical control from the early twentieth century, fraud detection from the 1980s and 1990s, and the methods standard today. The worked example shows the calibration arithmetic: the squared gaps are 0.0025, 0.16, 0.0144, 0.09, and 0.0784, which sum to 0.3453, so the Brier score is 0.0691, and the mean predicted probability is 0.65, with 5 hits out of 5. The result that matters is the threshold: the scores separate cleanly here, with the anomalies above 0.7 and the normals below 0.45, and a threshold between them would catch all three anomalies with no false alarms on this small example. The demo prints these checked figures: brier: 0.0691, hits: 5, mean_p: 0.65, n: 5.

When It Applies

Anomaly detection applies wherever the rare event is expensive and normal is well observed: fraud, equipment failure, and unauthorized network access. It applies where the threshold is set with the users, the false alarms are counted, and the model adapts as normal drifts. It does not apply where normal is not stable, where the false alarms would swamp the users, or where an adversary can learn the boundary and stay inside it.

Limits

The limits are the limits of defining normal. A system that flags everything unusual will drown its users in false alarms, and a system that flags nothing will miss the event that matters. The threshold is a judgment about costs, not a fact about the data, and it must be set with the users who will act on the flags. An adversary can also learn the boundary and stay inside it, which is the fraudster's game. The honest account reports the catches, the false alarms, and the misses, and sets the threshold with the users, not for them.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the Brier score. The five predictions and outcomes give squared gaps of 0.0025, 0.16, 0.0144, 0.09, and 0.0784, which sum to 0.3453, and 0.3453 divided by 5 is 0.0691. The mean predicted probability is 3.25 divided by 5, which is 0.65, and 5 of the 5 predictions were hits. A Brier score near zero means the probabilities were well calibrated; the worked example's score of 0.0691 is good, and the clean separation between the anomaly scores and the normal scores is what a detection system hopes for. Formal statement: Brier = (1/n) * sum (p_i - o_i)^2, where p_i is the predicted probability and o_i is 1 when the event happened and 0 when it did not. The equation states the calibration check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted probabilities and outcomes, squares each gap, and averages them for the Brier score.

Science Proof

The science behind anomaly detection is the science of the normal: a model of normal behaviour is a model of what to expect, and an anomaly is a case the model did not expect. The science says the difficulty is the definition of normal, because normal changes, and a model that does not adapt will flag the new normal as anomalous. The methods work where normal is stable and well observed, and they fail where normal drifts, where the anomalies are subtle, or where the cost of a false alarm is high enough that the system is switched off. Scientific relation: A predicted probability is calibrated when events it calls likely actually happen often; the Brier score measures that gap, with 0 for perfect prediction.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
probs = [(0.95, 1), (0.4, 0), (0.88, 1), (0.3, 0), (0.72, 1)]
def compute():
    brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
    n = len(probs)
    out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRED000020:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        probs = [(0.95, 1), (0.4, 0), (0.88, 1), (0.3, 0), (0.72, 1)]
        def compute():
            brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
            mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
            hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
            n = len(probs)
            out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}

Bias and Fairness: The COMPAS Lesson JAH-PRED-000021 · 21

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Bias in predictive models is the problem that the data remembers the past, including its injustices, and the model learns them faithfully. The standing example is COMPAS, the risk-assessment tool used in American courts, which the news organization ProPublica analyzed in 2016 and found to be biased against Black defendants: it falsely flagged them as future criminals at almost twice the rate it falsely flagged white defendants. The vendor disputed the analysis, and the debate that followed taught the field its central lesson: a model can be accurate on average and unfair in its errors, and the fairness of the errors must be measured, not assumed.

History

The history runs from the early awareness of bias in the 1970s and 1980s, through the spread of risk assessment in criminal justice, to ProPublica's 2016 analysis of COMPAS, which made the issue public and concrete. The analysis found that the tool's false-positive rate, the rate at which people who did not reoffend were flagged as high risk, was about twice as high for Black defendants as for white defendants. The vendor, Northpointe, replied that the tool was calibrated, meaning that a given score meant the same risk for both groups, and the debate showed that calibration and equal error rates cannot both hold when the base rates differ, which is a mathematical fact, not a matter of opinion. The field's response was fairness metrics, auditing, and the recognition that the choice between fairness definitions is a policy choice, not a technical one.

Description

Bias in predictive models is the problem that the data remembers the past, including its injustices, and the model learns them faithfully. The standing example is COMPAS, the risk-assessment tool used in American courts, which the news organization ProPublica analyzed in 2016 and found to be biased against Black defendants: it falsely flagged them as future criminals at almost twice the rate it falsely flagged white defendants. The vendor disputed the analysis, and the debate that followed taught the field its central lesson: a model can be accurate on average and unfair in its errors, and the fairness of the errors must be measured, not assumed.

The Problem

What is bias and fairness in predictive models, and the 2016 ProPublica analysis of COMPAS, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the model's predictions and the outcomes, split by group. Step 2: compute the error rates for each group, the false positives and the false negatives. Step 3: compare the rates across groups, and ask whether the differences are acceptable. Step 4: report the rates, the calibration, and the trade-off, and let the policy decide. The demo runs a small worked example of the comparison step: it takes five group error rates, 0.22, 0.18, 0.25, 0.2, and 0.21, against reference rates of 0.2, 0.2, 0.2, 0.2, and 0.2, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the comparison arithmetic, not a record of the COMPAS data. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"propublica_year": 2016, "mae_worked": 0.02, "rmse_worked": 0.0261, "max_gap_worked": 0.05}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates and the finding: ProPublica's 2016 analysis, and the false-positive rate about twice as high for Black defendants as for white defendants. The worked example shows the comparison arithmetic: the gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.05, 0.0, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.02 and a root mean square error of 0.0265, with a largest gap of 0.05. The result that matters is the method: fairness is measured by comparing error rates across groups, and a difference of five points in a false-positive rate is a difference that must be explained, not ignored. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.02, max_err: 0.05, n: 5, rmse: 0.0261.

When It Applies

Bias and fairness apply as an audit discipline to every predictive model that affects people: credit, hiring, criminal justice, and any decision where the errors fall unevenly. They apply where the error rates are measured by group, the trade-off is stated, and the policy decides. They do not apply as a single metric, they do not apply where the groups are not defined, and they do not apply where the audit is performed by the model's own builders without independence.

Limits

The limits are the limits of measurement itself. A fairness audit can measure the errors, but it cannot choose between fairness definitions, because the choice is a value judgment. The audit also depends on the groups being defined and the data being available, and it can be gamed by choosing the definition that makes the model look best. The honest account reports several fairness metrics, states the trade-off, and lets the policy decide, because a technical paper cannot settle a question of justice.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.05, 0.0, and 0.01, which sum to 0.1, so the mean absolute error is 0.1 divided by 5, which is 0.02. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0025, 0.0, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.0034, so the mean squared error is 0.00068 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00068, which is 0.0261. The largest single gap is 0.05. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the fairness; the fairness was proved, or not, by the audit of the real system. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind fairness is the science of error distribution: a model's errors are not spread evenly, and the unevenness follows the inequalities in the data. The impossibility result states the tension: when the base rates differ between groups, a model cannot be both calibrated and have equal error rates, so a choice must be made, and the choice is a policy decision. The science says the measurement must come first, because a bias that is not measured cannot be debated, and the debate must be public, because the choice affects people who did not choose the model. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.22, 0.2), (0.18, 0.2), (0.25, 0.2), (0.2, 0.2), (0.21, 0.2)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Concept Drift: When the World Moves On JAH-PRED-000022 · 22

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Concept drift is the slow failure of a predictive model: the world changes, the relationship the model learned no longer holds, and the predictions quietly get worse. It happens everywhere, because customers change, markets change, diseases change, and the data that trained the model is a snapshot of a past that no longer exists. The defence is monitoring: measure the model's performance continuously, watch the input distribution for change, and retrain when the drift is detected. A model without monitoring is a model that will fail silently, and silent failure is the worst kind.

History

The history runs from the early recognition that models decay, through the twentieth century, when the practice was to rebuild models on a schedule, to the present, where drift detection is automated and retraining is continuous. The lesson was learned in production, where models that were accurate at launch degraded over months, and the degradation was only noticed when the damage was done. The modern practice pairs the model with a monitor, which tracks the error rate and the input distribution, and raises the alarm when either moves. The discipline is now standard, and the models that survive are the ones that are watched.

Description

Concept drift is the slow failure of a predictive model: the world changes, the relationship the model learned no longer holds, and the predictions quietly get worse. It happens everywhere, because customers change, markets change, diseases change, and the data that trained the model is a snapshot of a past that no longer exists. The defence is monitoring: measure the model's performance continuously, watch the input distribution for change, and retrain when the drift is detected. A model without monitoring is a model that will fail silently, and silent failure is the worst kind.

The Problem

What is concept drift, model decay over time, and the monitoring that catches it, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the model's predictions and the outcomes as they arrive, and measure the error continuously. Step 2: watch the input distribution for change, comparing the recent inputs with the training inputs. Step 3: when the error rises or the distribution shifts beyond a threshold, flag the drift. Step 4: retrain the model on recent data, and report the before and after. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five monthly error rates, 0.12, 0.14, 0.18, 0.23, and 0.29, against a baseline rate of 0.12, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the drift arithmetic, not a record of a historical model. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_worked": 0.072, "rmse_worked": 0.0949, "max_gap_worked": 0.17}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and its documentation: drift detection standard in production machine learning, and retraining the standard remedy. The worked example shows the drift arithmetic: the gaps from the baseline are 0.0, 0.02, 0.06, 0.11, and 0.17, giving a mean absolute error of 0.072 and a root mean square error of 0.0949, with a largest gap of 0.17. The result that matters is the trend: the error rises every month, from 0.12 to 0.29, which is the signature of drift, and the rise is steady enough that a monitor would have flagged it by the third month. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.072, max_err: 0.17, n: 5, rmse: 0.0949.

When It Applies

Concept drift applies as a monitoring discipline to every model in production: measure the error continuously, watch the inputs, and retrain when the drift is detected. It applies where the monitor is part of the system, where the threshold is set with the users, and where the retraining data is checked. It does not apply as a one-time test, it does not apply where the model is never monitored, and it does not apply where the retraining would import the very bias that caused the drift.

Limits

The limits are the limits of detection. Drift can be slow enough to hide inside the noise, and a monitor that alarms too often will be ignored, while one that alarms too rarely will miss the drift. Retraining on recent data can also import the drift's cause, if the recent data is itself biased, and a model retrained too often will chase noise. The honest account sets the threshold with the users, reports the drift when it is found, and retrains on data that has been checked, because a retrained model is only as good as the data it was retrained on.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.0, 0.02, 0.06, 0.11, and 0.17, which sum to 0.36, so the mean absolute error is 0.36 divided by 5, which is 0.072. The squared gaps are 0.0, 0.0004, 0.0036, 0.0121, and 0.0289, which sum to 0.045, so the mean squared error is 0.009 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.009, which is 0.0949. The largest single gap is 0.17. The root mean square error exceeds the mean absolute error because the later gaps are larger, which is the signature of a rising trend. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind drift is the science of non-stationarity: the relationship between inputs and outcomes changes over time, and a model fitted to the past will fail on the future to the extent that the relationship has changed. The science distinguishes the kinds of drift: sudden, when a policy changes overnight; gradual, when behaviour shifts slowly; and recurring, when the pattern returns with the season. The detection methods watch the error and the inputs, and the remedy is retraining, because a model cannot adapt to a change it has not seen. The science says the monitor is part of the model, not an addition to it. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

The checkable results are the practice and its documentation: drift detection standard in production machine learning, and retraining the standard remedy. The worked example shows the drift arithmetic: the gaps from the baseline are 0.0, 0.02, 0.06, 0.11, and 0.17, giving a mean absolute error of 0.072 and a root mean square error of 0.0949, with a largest gap of 0.17. The result that matters is the trend: the error rises every month, from 0.12 to 0.29, which is the signature of drift, and the rise is steady enough that a monitor would have flagged it by the third month. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.072, max_err: 0.17, n: 5, rmse: 0.0949.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of detection. Drift can be slow enough to hide inside the noise, and a monitor that alarms too often will be ignored, while one that alarms too rarely will miss the drift. Retraining on recent data can also import the drift's cause, if the recent data is itself biased, and a model retrained too often will chase noise. The honest account sets the threshold with the users, reports the drift when it is found, and retrains on data that has been checked, because a retrained model is only as good as the data it was retrained on.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.12, 0.12), (0.14, 0.12), (0.18, 0.12), (0.23, 0.12), (0.29, 0.12)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Probabilistic Forecasting: Prediction Intervals JAH-PRED-000023 · 23

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Probabilistic forecasting is prediction with the uncertainty attached: instead of a single number, give a range, and say how likely the truth is to fall inside it. A prediction interval says the value will fall between these bounds with, say, 90 percent probability, and a quantile forecast says the value will fall below this level with a stated probability. The methods are quantile regression and ensemble forecasting, and the discipline is calibration: an interval that claims 90 percent coverage must cover the truth about 90 percent of the time. A point forecast without its interval is a promise without its risk, and the interval is the honest part.

History

The history runs from the early interval forecasts in statistics, through the twentieth century, when prediction intervals became standard in economics and weather, to the present, where probabilistic forecasting is the standard in energy, finance, and weather. The weather services led the way, with ensemble forecasts that show the range of possible futures, and the energy industry followed, because a forecast of demand without its uncertainty cannot guide a grid. The discipline is now standard: report the interval, check the coverage, and recalibrate when the coverage is wrong.

Description

Probabilistic forecasting is prediction with the uncertainty attached: instead of a single number, give a range, and say how likely the truth is to fall inside it. A prediction interval says the value will fall between these bounds with, say, 90 percent probability, and a quantile forecast says the value will fall below this level with a stated probability. The methods are quantile regression and ensemble forecasting, and the discipline is calibration: an interval that claims 90 percent coverage must cover the truth about 90 percent of the time. A point forecast without its interval is a promise without its risk, and the interval is the honest part.

The Problem

What is probabilistic forecasting, prediction intervals, and quantile regression, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the forecast and produce a distribution, not a point, by quantile regression or by an ensemble. Step 2: read the prediction interval from the distribution, for example the 5th to the 95th percentile for a 90 percent interval. Step 3: when the actuals arrive, check how often they fall inside the interval. Step 4: recalibrate if the coverage differs from the stated probability. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five interval widths, 12, 15, 10, 18, and 14 units, against a target width of 12, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the comparison arithmetic, not a record of a historical forecast. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_worked": 2.6, "rmse_worked": 3.2558, "max_gap_worked": 6}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and its documentation: prediction intervals standard in weather, energy, and finance, and coverage the standard check. The worked example shows the comparison arithmetic: the gaps are 0, 3, 2, 6, and 2, giving a mean absolute error of 2.6 and a root mean square error of 3.2558, with a largest gap of 6. The result that matters is the interpretation: an interval that is too wide is safe but useless, and one that is too narrow is precise but wrong, so the width must be judged against the coverage, not alone. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.6, max_err: 6, n: 5, rmse: 3.2558.

When It Applies

Probabilistic forecasting applies wherever a decision depends on the risk, not only on the most likely outcome: energy, finance, weather, and any prediction where the range matters. It applies where the interval is reported, the coverage is checked, and the recalibration is done. It does not apply where a single number is presented as certain, where the coverage is never checked, or where the interval is so wide that it says nothing.

Limits

The limits are the limits of any distribution. A prediction interval is only as good as the model that produced it, and a model that underestimates the uncertainty will produce intervals that are too narrow. The coverage check needs data, and a short history cannot prove the coverage. And an interval can be calibrated on average and wrong in the cases that matter, as in a crisis, where the distribution itself changes. The honest account reports the interval with its coverage, and never presents a point forecast as if it were certain.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0, 3, 2, 6, and 2, which sum to 13, so the mean absolute error is 13 divided by 5, which is 2.6. The squared gaps are 0, 9, 4, 36, and 4, which sum to 53, so the mean squared error is 10.6 and the root mean square error is the square root of 10.6, which is 3.2558. The largest single gap is 6. The root mean square error exceeds the mean absolute error because the gap of 6 dominates the squares, which is the signature of one interval that is much wider than the target. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind probabilistic forecasting is the science of uncertainty as a quantity: the future is not a number but a distribution, and the forecast's job is to describe that distribution honestly. Quantile regression estimates the distribution directly, and ensembles estimate it by running the model many times with different starting states. The science says the interval must be calibrated, because an interval that claims 90 percent coverage and covers 70 percent is a lie, and the coverage check is the test that catches it. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

The Limits of Predictive AI: Black Swans and Goodhart JAH-PRED-000024 · 24

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Predictive AI fails in ways that matter, and the failures have names. A black swan is an event outside the model's experience, which the model cannot foresee because it has never seen one. Goodhart's law states that when a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure, because people will game it. The 2008 financial crisis showed both at once: the risk models had never seen a nationwide housing crash, so they did not foresee one, and the measures they optimized were gamed until they meant nothing. These are not edge cases but the central failure modes of prediction, and the honest account of predictive AI starts with them.

History

The history runs from the early warnings, through the 2008 crisis, which made the failure modes concrete, to the present, where black swans and Goodhart's law are standard terms in the field. Nassim Nicholas Taleb's 2007 book The Black Swan gave the first its name, and Goodhart's law, stated by Charles Goodhart in 1975, gave the second its. The crisis showed the two together: the models were fitted to a history without a nationwide housing crash, so the crash was a black swan, and the measures of risk were targets, so they were gamed until they stopped measuring risk. The field's response was stress testing, scenario analysis, and the recognition that a model is only as good as the history it was fitted to.

Description

Predictive AI fails in ways that matter, and the failures have names. A black swan is an event outside the model's experience, which the model cannot foresee because it has never seen one. Goodhart's law states that when a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure, because people will game it. The 2008 financial crisis showed both at once: the risk models had never seen a nationwide housing crash, so they did not foresee one, and the measures they optimized were gamed until they meant nothing. These are not edge cases but the central failure modes of prediction, and the honest account of predictive AI starts with them.

The Problem

What is the limits and failure modes of predictive AI, black swans, Goodhart's law, and the 2008 crisis, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take a model and ask what it has never seen, the events outside its training history. Step 2: ask what would happen if the measure it optimizes became a target, and how it would be gamed. Step 3: stress-test the model on scenarios outside its history, and report where it breaks. Step 4: keep a human in the loop for the cases the model cannot judge. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five stress scenarios with model errors of 8, 12, 6, 15, and 9 units, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical stress test. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"goodhart_year": 1975, "taleb_year": 2007, "crisis_year": 2008, "total_error_worked": 50, "mean_error_worked": 10.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Goodhart's law stated 1975, Taleb's book 2007, and the financial crisis 2008. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five scenarios give errors of 8, 12, 6, 15, and 9, for a total of 50, a mean of 10.0, a smallest of 6, and a largest of 15. The result that matters is the size: a mean error of ten units in stress scenarios, against a normal error of two or three, is the signature of a model that works in normal times and fails in the times that matter, which is the failure mode the stress test is meant to find. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 15, mean: 10.0, n: 5, smallest: 6, total: 50.

When It Applies

The limits apply as a discipline to every predictive model: name the black swans, name the gaming, stress-test outside the history, and report the stress errors. They apply to finance, medicine, and any prediction where the failure would be expensive. They do not apply as a reason to abandon prediction, because a model that is honest about its limits is still useful, and they do not apply as an excuse to deploy a model whose stress errors were never measured.

Limits

The limits are the whole subject of this record, so they are stated plainly. Predictive AI cannot foresee what it has never seen, it will be gamed when its measure becomes a target, and it will fail in a crisis exactly when it is needed most. Its errors are largest in the cases that matter, because those are the cases outside its history. The honest account stress-tests every model, reports the stress errors alongside the normal ones, and keeps a human in the loop for the cases the model cannot judge.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five scenarios give errors of 8, 12, 6, 15, and 9, which sum to 50, and 50 divided by 5 is 10.0. The smallest error is 6 and the largest is 15. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the point concrete: stress errors are several times the normal errors, and a model whose stress errors are never measured is a model whose failure mode is unknown. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind the limits is the science of induction: a model learns from the past, and the past does not contain the future's novelties. A black swan is an event outside the training distribution, and no amount of fitting to the past can foresee it. Goodhart's law is the science of incentives: a measure that becomes a target will be optimized, and the optimization will destroy the measure's meaning. The science says the remedy is not a better model but a different practice: stress testing, scenario analysis, and human judgment for the cases the model cannot judge. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. History

The history runs from the early warnings, through the 2008 crisis, which made the failure modes concrete, to the present, where black swans and Goodhart's law are standard terms in the field. Nassim Nicholas Taleb's 2007 book The Black Swan gave the first its name, and Goodhart's law, stated by Charles Goodhart in 1975, gave the second its. The crisis showed the two together: the models were fitted to a history without a nationwide housing crash, so the crash was a black swan, and the measures of risk were targets, so they were gamed until they stopped measuring risk. The field's response was stress testing, scenario analysis, and the recognition that a model is only as good as the history it was fitted to.

Problem

What is the limits and failure modes of predictive AI, black swans, Goodhart's law, and the 2008 crisis, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: take a model and ask what it has never seen, the events outside its training history. Step 2: ask what would happen if the measure it optimizes became a target, and how it would be gamed. Step 3: stress-test the model on scenarios outside its history, and report where it breaks. Step 4: keep a human in the loop for the cases the model cannot judge. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five stress scenarios with model errors of 8, 12, 6, 15, and 9 units, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical stress test.

Dataset

Dataset: limits of predictive AI; Goodhart's law 1975; The Black Swan 2007; financial crisis 2008. Demo: worked example totaling model errors across five stress scenarios. Provenance: public-harvest from the cited public sources; licensing: public facts and figures, freely reusable, with sources cited. Last verified 2026-11-16.

Formal equation

total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts

Formal equation, in plain words

The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

The checkable results are the dates: Goodhart's law stated 1975, Taleb's book 2007, and the financial crisis 2008. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five scenarios give errors of 8, 12, 6, 15, and 9, for a total of 50, a mean of 10.0, a smallest of 6, and a largest of 15. The result that matters is the size: a mean error of ten units in stress scenarios, against a normal error of two or three, is the signature of a model that works in normal times and fails in the times that matter, which is the failure mode the stress test is meant to find. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 15, mean: 10.0, n: 5, smallest: 6, total: 50.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the whole subject of this record, so they are stated plainly. Predictive AI cannot foresee what it has never seen, it will be gamed when its measure becomes a target, and it will fail in a crisis exactly when it is needed most. Its errors are largest in the cases that matter, because those are the cases outside its history. The honest account stress-tests every model, reports the stress errors alongside the normal ones, and keeps a human in the loop for the cases the model cannot judge.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('scenario 1', 8), ('scenario 2', 12), ('scenario 3', 6), ('scenario 4', 15), ('scenario 5', 9)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRED000024:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('scenario 1', 8), ('scenario 2', 12), ('scenario 3', 6), ('scenario 4', 15), ('scenario 5', 9)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRED000024().run())
